# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-04

| **[HarmoHOI: Harmonizing Appearance and 3D Motion for Multi-view Hand-Object Interaction Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17097v1)** | 2026-07-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hand-Object Interaction (HOI) synthesis is a cornerstone for animation production and embodied AI. Despite the strong priors of video foundation models, multi-view consistent HOI synthesis remains challenging due to complex hand motions and occlusions. We present HarmoHOI, a unified diffusion framework that jointly and harmoniously generates synchronized multi-view HOI videos and globally aligned 3D point tracks. Our core insight is that robust multi-view consistency fundamentally requires globally aligned 3D geometry and motion. To this end, we propose a Mixture of Multi-view Diffusion Transformer that co-models RGB videos and 3D point tracks. By representing point tracks as pseudo-videos, we align 3D geometric signals with the 2D latent space of foundation models, thereby minimizing the domain gap and easing adaptation of priors. To further ensure geometry consistency, we introduce Global Motion Aligning Diffusion, which refines coarse point tracks into metric-scale, globally aligned 3D trajectories. HarmoHOI enables on-the-fly co-evolution of 2D appearance and 3D motion during denoising. To overcome the scarcity of multi-view HOI data, we employ a hybrid data curriculum learning strategy that successfully transfers generic priors from single-view data to synchronized multi-view generation. Experimental results show that HarmoHOI achieves state-of-the-art performance in visual quality, motion plausibility, and multi-view geometric consistency. Project page available at https://droliven.github.io/HarmoHOI_project.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Perception to Assistance: Open-Vocabulary Shared Autonomy for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17323v1)** | 2026-07-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teleoperating a robotic manipulator in industrial environments demands precision that camera-based interfaces alone struggle to deliver. The operator must align the end-effector with a target in clutter, under limited depth perception, and without colliding with the surrounding structures. This paper presents a shared-autonomy framework that assists the operator throughout this process. A single RGB-D camera captures the operator's arm motion and hand gestures without wearables, fiducials, or a calibration stage. The intended target is specified by a free-form text prompt, grounded by a vision-language model in the robot's gripper camera, and tracked across its onboard cameras by a promptable video-segmentation model, resulting in a grasp frame continuously separated from the obstacle map. Every commanded motion is executed by a GPU-accelerated model-predictive controller that enforces self- and environment-collision avoidance against an online volumetric reconstruction, while a potential field corrects the operator's reference toward the grounded target during the final approach. An autonomous mode can be gesture-triggered to complete the grasp on the same target without a separate perception pipeline. The framework is validated on a quadruped mobile manipulator. The interface achieves a positional RMSE of 59 mm relative to motion-capture ground truth, and the controller keeps the arm at least 18 cm from obstacles while the operator deliberately commands the arm into them by 6 cm. In an industrial valve manipulation and a pick-and-place task, the full framework succeeded in all trials, while ablating either the collision or the assistance module produced failures through complementary mechanisms, and autonomous execution succeeded in four of five trials per task.</p></details> |  |
| **[Retriever: Composing Closed-Loop Asynchronous Robot Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17213v1)** | 2026-07-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Building long-horizon robot agents requires composing closed-loop pipelines -- perception, belief update, planning, and control -- whose components run at different clocks and with variable latency. Today, these systems are often assembled with ad-hoc concurrency and pub/sub conventions that make timing and input-consumption semantics implicit, yielding schedule-dependent behavior that is hard to reproduce, debug, and reuse. Current solutions typically solve parts of this problem at either the algorithmic or the systems level, but not both. In this work, we propose Retriever, which spans the entire stack: an asynchronous decision model, a programming model, a runtime, and an example closed-loop agent pipeline. Retriever represents an agent as a graph of stateful causal stream functions executed on explicit run clocks. We formalize this view via an asynchronous environment-agent loop over continuous-time streams and show that finite-memory causal policies can be represented by compositions of these operators. Retriever compiles these graphs into a runtime that supports multiple backends, enabling systematic debugging across running environments and deterministic replay from logged asynchronous data. We evaluate Retriever through a real-robot case study together with controlled studies of runtime overhead and deterministic replay behavior.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: http://retriever.systems; Package open-source website: http://openretriever.org</p></details> |
| **[VersualRL: Closed-Loop Verbal Reinforcement Learning with Visual Execution Feedback for Task-Level Robot Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22169v3)** | 2026-07-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce VersualRL, a closed-loop framework for task-level robot planning that uses visual execution feedback to iteratively refine executable Behavior Trees through structured natural-language critique. VersualRL combines a vision-language model critic, which analyzes visual observations and Behavior Team execution traces, with a large language model actor that performs discrete and interpretable policy updates. During physical deployment, both foundation models remain frozen, while the executed Behavior Tree is updated at the symbolic level without online gradient-based policy optimization. This enables transparent reasoning, explicit causal feedback, and human-interpretable policy evolution. We validate VersualRL on a real mobile robot performing a multi-stage navigation and manipulation task under execution uncertainty. Experimental results show that the framework supports explainable policy improvements, closed-loop adaptation to execution failures, and successful deployment on physical robotic systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS) 2026</p></details> |
| **[Articulated Humanoid Head for a Robot Receptionist Capable of Natural Human Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17042v1)** | 2026-07-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humanoid robots have become increasingly popular in applications such as social interaction, education, and service roles, which drives the need for more natural and efficient human-robot interactions. However, currently available humanoid heads often face limitations, including high costs, mechanical complexity, and limited adaptability across diverse environments. To address these challenges, we present an articulated humanoid robot head designed for a receptionist role, integrating a mechanical structure with 21 degrees of freedom (DoF), including mechanisms for the mouth, eyes, eyebrows, and neck, and covered with realistic silicone skin to achieve a human-like appearance and expression. The system integrates a model-based architecture that combines SCRFD, ArcFace, and ByTetrack for face recognition and Llama and Whisper for natural language processing, with hardware support enabling real-time operations and human re-identification. The conversational ability and re-identification capabilities of the humanoid robot head were quantitatively measured, while its emotional expressiveness and human likeness were evaluated through a user study, achieving an average human likeness score of 4.13 out of 5.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This work is accepted at IEEE/ASME International Conference on Advanced Intelligent Mechatronics (AIM2026)</p></details> |
| **[Stochastic Entanglement of Deterministic Origami Tentacles For Robust Robotic Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26897v2)** | 2026-07-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Origami-inspired robotic grippers have shown promising potential for object manipulation tasks due to their compact volume and mechanical flexibility. However, robust capture of objects with random shapes in dynamic working environments often comes at the cost of additional actuation channels and control complexity. Here, we introduce a tendon-driven, robust origami tentacle gripper by exploiting a synergy between local, deterministic deformation programming and global, stochastic entanglements. Each tentacle features carefully placed holes (for routing an actuation tendon), origami creases, and a tapered shape. By tailoring these design features, one can prescribe the shrinking, bending, and twisting deformation, eventually creating deterministic coiling with a simple tendon pull. Then, when multiple coiling tentacles are placed in proximity, stochastic entanglement emerges, allowing the tentacles to braid, knot, and grip objects with random shapes. We derived a simulation model by integrating origami mechanics with Cosserat rods to correlate origami design, tentacle deformation, and collective grasping performance. Then, we experimentally tested how these entangling origami tentacles can grasp objects under gravity and in water. A stow-and-release deployment mechanism was also tested to simulate in-orbit grasping. Overall, this entanglement-enabled tentacle gripper presents a route toward robust object grasping with simple design and actuation.</p></details> |  |

## Embodied AI
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Embodied AI for Scientific Discovery: From Physical Action to Scientific Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04371v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific experiments are increasingly selected, executed and interpreted by learned systems that act through physical interfaces. Robot learning builds physical competence, whereas AI for science chooses which experiment to run, each reporting different endpoints. This review treats their interface as one problem, embodied AI for science, where the experiment itself is partially observed: specimen state, apparatus state and measurement processes are latent, actions are costly and irreversible, and missingness depends on execution and outcome. We organize the literature by three closures operating at distinct levels: operational closure (the experiment realized is the one intended), epistemic closure (the result updates beliefs or selects subsequent experiments) and evidential closure (the record supports a claim). We analyze workflows through a record-linked evidence cycle - Record, Diagnose, Calibrate, Confirm - as analytic functions with feedback. A taxonomy crosses these closures with six delegated decisions and five embodiment classes. Our formulation separates commanded, realized and observed conditions and models diagnosis with explicit resource, risk and sample-change costs. We compare experiment-selection methods by terminal decision, review perception, protocol compilation, visuomotor policies, world models, recovery and transfer by evidence emission requirements, and compare chemistry, materials, biology, microscopy and field workflows by what their records establish. An appendix provides an evidence map of 28 workflows (26 physical, two software-boundary), characterizing validation pathways and claim confirmation requirements. We propose evaluation that separates actions, valid experiments, campaigns and confirmed claims, framing open challenges as controlled comparisons with measurable failure conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>46 pa...</summary><p>46 pages, 10 figures, 14 tables. Review article</p></details> |
| **[RoboScope Kit: Unified Interfaces for Fast and Scalable Embodied-AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03850v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Simulation benchmarks are the standard way to compare robot policies, but each benchmark brings its own evaluator, observation layout, and action convention, and evaluation is slow: 500 RoboTwin Easy episodes with one simulator and one model on one GPU take 359.7 minutes. Profiling the official StarVLA evaluator shows that simulation occupies CPU cores while the GPU averages 14.3% utilization. RoboScope Kit wraps each model once in a Python class called a Policy, and each benchmark once in an adapter around the benchmark's own evaluator, so the benchmark's rules and scoring stay unchanged. Scheduling runs simulator workers and Policy replicas as separate pools, sizes both from measured probes, and shards work deterministically across GPU instances; runtime optimization renders only the observations an action-chunk Policy reads. Adapters cover six benchmark families. On LIBERO-Long with a StarVLA checkpoint on one 48 GiB RTX 4090, the concurrency search selects twelve simulator workers sharing three Policy replicas, which evaluate 500 episodes 4.45$\times$ faster than the official StarVLA evaluation with equivalent success rates, 2.78$\times$ faster than RLinf, and 1.52$\times$ faster than vla-eval at its tuned configuration. With CUDA MPS, which lets the replicas run their kernels concurrently, the search selects sixteen workers sharing four replicas, 6.16$\times$ faster than StarVLA, 3.85$\times$ faster than RLinf, and 2.10$\times$ faster than vla-eval. On RoboTwin Easy, four GPUs with chunk-boundary observation complete the 500 episodes in 14.4 minutes, 25.04$\times$ faster than the single-GPU baseline, with identical success outcomes and terminal steps.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 9 figures. Project page: https://roboscope.pages.dev/ Code: https://github.com/MINT-SJTU/RoboScope-Kit</p></details> |
| **[Labference: Benchmarking the Reasoning-Action Connection in Robotic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35252v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realizing Embodied AI for Science requires robots to translate experimental goals into reliable physical procedures, reducing hazardous and repetitive human work while generating evidence for scientific discovery. Although language-based planning and learned manipulation provide complementary capabilities, their integration requires robots to adapt subsequent operations to actual execution outcomes. For example, an unsuccessful placement changes what must be done next, even when the experimental goal remains unchanged. Existing benchmarks assess planning, manipulation, and laboratory workflows through task completion and reasoning or execution metrics; however, aggregate performance leaves the connection between outcome-dependent choices and physical execution unresolved. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce Labference, a benchmark for evaluating how observed physical outcomes guide subsequent choices and how those choices translate into robotic laboratory actions. Its evaluation protocol is designed to test outcome-dependent behavioral adaptation through controlled evidence variations, while independently measuring operation selection, physical execution, and task completion. Matched Oracle-assisted continuations from shared checkpoints then assess which forms of support improve subsequent choices and task completion. Initial weighing evaluations reveal target-selection errors in the tested VLA policies despite successful object transfers, whereas GPT-driven control achieves correct selection but encounters execution failures and substantial computation costs. Together, these findings highlight the need to connect task-appropriate reasoning with reliable, timely action. By making this connection measurable and diagnosable, Labference supports targeted improvements toward autonomous experimentation for Embodied AI for Science.</p></details> | <details><summary>Updat...</summary><p>Update project address</p></details> |
| **[When the Past Shapes Action: Understanding Embodied Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41688v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in generative foundation models have expanded the capabilities of embodied AI, enabling increasingly general navigation, manipulation, and long-horizon task execution. However, effective interaction with dynamic environments requires more than only strong perception and reasoning: agents must act with partial knowledge and retain, retrieve, and update information from past observations, actions, and experiences. This survey examines embodied memory as a mechanism for preserving decision-relevant information beyond the current observation. We organize existing approaches along two complementary dimensions: six fundamental memory requirements, spanning world-state persistence, task progress, belief maintenance, and experiential learning; and eight architectural categories, including recurrent states, fast weights, visual and symbolic representations, spatial memory, and experience-based systems. We analyze their capabilities and limitations, and examine how existing benchmarks evaluate memory-dependent behavior, distinguishing genuine use of historical information from general task performance. Finally, we identify open challenges in selecting relevant experiences, maintaining consistent beliefs in changing environments, and consolidating knowledge across episodes. Our analysis provides a framework for understanding memory as an essential component of reliable, adaptive, and lifelong-horizon embodied intelligence.</p></details> |  |
| **[Gym-Anything for Robotics: Scaling physical simulations with digital agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40565v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realistic, fast robotics simulations could enable large-scale training of embodied AI models that transfer to the real world. However, despite decades of progress, available simulations cover only a narrow range of real-world tasks and physical phenomena. The core bottleneck is that creating better simulations requires significant expert effort and therefore does not scale. To address this, we introduce Gym-Anything for Robotics, a framework in which coding agents create realistic robotics simulations. Our core insight is that simulation creation is a coding agent task, so improvements in coding agents translate directly into richer and more faithful simulations. We select simulations based on U.S. GDP data to cover economically valuable physical work, define what makes a good simulation and a good task, and develop an adversarial creation-audit loop that checks each simulation and task against these definitions. Using this pipeline, we build RoboWorld, a set of 25 simulations with 25 test and 175 training RL environments of varying difficulty. Even the best frontier models fail on every RoboWorld test task, achieving an average reward of only 0.06. Building on RoboWorld, we introduce several new ways for frontier models to improve, including in-context learning across repeated attempts, a privileged teacher that reads the simulator state and teaches a student in context, self-assisted learning from the model's own privileged experience, and code policies that generate demonstrations across varied scenes to train vision-language-action models (VLAs) such as $π_{0.5}$. Each of these substantially improves performance. We release our pipeline, RL environments, and benchmark to facilitate future research. Code and data are available at https://gym-anything.github.io/robotics</p></details> |  |
| **[UniffordInteractor: Reasoning the Unified Affordance for Human and Multi-Object Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39745v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deriving plausible and dynamic Human Object Interaction (HOI) has gained tremendous progress in Embodied AI and Immersive AR/VR. While existing HOI methods have effectively explored human interaction with individual or fixed objects, they still show limitations in complex multi-object interaction scenes. Meanwhile, previous approaches focus more on the contact information held by human-object relation, overlooking the significance of the contact situation of various entities (i.e., object-object). To address these limitations, we propose UniffordInteractor, a Reasoning-then-Interacting framework that leverages unified affordance for realistic and long-horizon human and multi-object interaction modeling. At the reasoning stage, we devise a Unified Affordance Reasoner (UAR) that employs a fine-tuned 3D Multimodal Large Language Model for unified object-centric affordance prediction. At the interacting stage, we propose Hierarchical Interaction Generator (HIG), which formulates the interaction generation as a hierarchical (e.g., root to body to hand to objects) autoregressive prediction, conditioned on the obtained unified affordance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that UniffordInteractor surpasses SOTA counterparts in human and multi-object interaction generation.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 11 figures. Published in ACM Transactions on Graphics (Proceedings of SIGGRAPH Asia 2026)</p></details> |
| **[OmniDex: Scaling Dexterous Hand Grasping to Diverse Cluttered Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11194v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dexterous grasping is the foundational primitive in embodied AI, demanding massive data to train robust models. As real-world data collection is expensive, simulation has become the mainstream paradigm. Yet, while cluttered scenes best reflect real-world applications, learning to grasp within them is bottlenecked by a critical scarcity of large-scale data. To resolve this, we curate high-quality 3D objects and supporting bases, proposing a scalable seed-and-filter strategy that bypasses sluggish scene-level optimization. This yields an unprecedented benchmark comprising over 2.6 million scenes and 0.4B scene-specific grasp ground truths, featuring diverse realistic layouts paired with rich semantic and geometric observations. Furthermore, we introduce the OmniDex model to overcome the grasp multimodality and last-millimeter precision errors plaguing current generative models. By coupling Soft Winner-Takes-All learning with human-inspired physical constraints during training, and utilizing physics-driven ranking, our approach achieves robust dexterous grasping without the latency of post-optimization. Experimental results show that OmniDex model achieves state-of-the-art performance and strong generalization across diverse scenes, views, and unseen objects.</p></details> |  |
| **[InHabit: Scaling 3D Human-Scene Interaction Data With Image Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19673v3)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI and human-centric 3D scene understanding require data of people meaningfully interacting with diverse environments, yet such data remains scarce. Real-world capture has steadily pushed toward more scenes, but is bound to studios and at most around a hundred scenes, while synthetic datasets scale easily but place people without regard to what the scene affords. We argue that useful human-scene interaction data requires diversity in both scenes and interactions, and we show this with our proposed data engine InHabit. InHabit is a fully automatic engine that renders an existing 3D scene, uses 2D foundation models to propose and depict context-appropriate interactions, and lifts them into SMPL-X bodies, where the known scene geometry makes lifting well-constrained. Applied to Habitat-Matterport3D, InHabit produces InHabitants, which pairs photorealistic images with SMPL-X bodies and complete scene geometry across ~6.6k rooms, about 60x more scenes than the largest captured dataset. We illustrate the quality and value of such large-scale human-scene interaction data through a variety of experiments. In a perceptual study, our interactions are preferred over two prior placement methods in 78% of cases. Across three tasks - 1) contact estimation, 2) human-scene reconstruction, and 3) generating humans in scenes - training with InHabitants outperforms training on synthetic data with exact ground truth (BEDLAM), motion-capture data (TRUMANS), and real RGB-D captures (PROX); e.g., GRAFT trained on InHabitants reaches a contact F1 of 0.594 on PROX, versus 0.492 when trained on PROX itself. Project page: https://virtualhumans.mpi-inf.mpg.de/inhabit/</p></details> |  |
| **[POD-RoPE: Rotary Position Encoding over Physical Observation Distributions for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33745v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied perception requires positional representations that capture both physical geometry and the spatial and temporal extent of observations. We introduce POD-RoPE (Physical Observation Distribution RoPE), which represents token observation footprints as distributions over camera centers, viewing directions, and sampling times. Under independent sampling, expected rotations encode relative relationships between distributions; when each distribution collapses to a single address, the construction recovers pointwise RoPE. The same rotation statistics support Q/K matching and coherence-dependent V/O transformations, allowing observation geometry to guide both information selection and content aggregation. Per-token pre-aggregation avoids increasing sequence length or processing pairs of observation nodes. We evaluate POD-RoPE across four robot policy architectures, simulation and real-world manipulation, and complementary reconstruction, depth-estimation, and novel-view-synthesis tasks. Integrating POD-RoPE into Fast-WAM improves mean success from 91.83% to 92.88% across all 50 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks, and from 32.5% to 52.5% on two real-robot tasks on Sharpa North. Controlled reconstruction ablations support the contributions of distribution encoding and V/O transformations. In H20 model-block benchmarks, POD-RoPE achieves median forward-plus-backward speedups of $1.91\times$ over P-RoPE and $5.34\times$ over RayRoPE, with a median latency overhead of 17.4% over Standard RoPE. These results support physical observation distributions as an effective and computationally efficient basis for geometry-aware attention.</p></details> | 21 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Zooming without Zooming v2: From Seeing Details to Connecting Evidence for Generalizable Multimodal Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37037v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal perception forms the foundation of multimodal intelligence. In particular, spatial perception and multimodal long-context understanding are two pivotal tasks that underpin embodied AI and multimodal agents. However, synthesizing targeted, high-quality training data for these tasks remains difficult and costly. Our key insight is that these tasks share an underlying capability, which we call connecting evidence: identifying relevant visual facts, binding them to the correct entities, recovering their relations, and composing them into an answer. Crucially, targeted training data are far easier to synthesize for this capability than for these tasks themselves. To strengthen this capability, we propose EvidenceConnect, an easy-to-implement data synthesis framework that uses zoomed views of related regions within a single image to generate questions requiring comparison, aggregation, or chaining of visual evidence. To ensure quality, a reference answer is retained only when independent VLMs agree on it, and region-ablation tests discard questions whose answer survives the removal of selected regions. We then train base VLMs on the resulting data with RLVR, obtaining Zooming without Zooming v2 (ZwZ2). Although the synthesized data are not tailored to any downstream benchmark, ZwZ2 improves fine-grained and spatial perception, with further gains on embodied spatial reasoning, multimodal long-context understanding, multimodal agent tasks, and general visual reasoning. These results establish EvidenceConnect as an effective framework for supervising evidence connection and suggest that strengthening this shared capability leads to more generalizable multimodal perception.</p></details> |  |
| **[AmaraSpatial-10K: A Spatially and Semantically Aligned 3D Dataset for Spatial Computing and Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23018v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Web-scale 3D asset collections are abundant but rarely ready to ingest without cleanup, suffering from arbitrary metric scaling, incorrect pivots, brittle geometry, and incomplete textures, defects that limit their use in embodied AI, robotics, and spatial computing. We present AmaraSpatial-10K, a dataset of 10,071 synthetic 3D assets standardised for direct ingestion. Each asset ships as a metric-scaled, deterministically anchored .glb with separated PBR maps, a convex collision hull, a paired reference image, and multi-sentence text metadata. Alongside the dataset we introduce a reusable evaluation suite for 3D asset banks, a continuous Scale Plausibility Score (SPS), a keyword-based Concept Density metric, anchor-error auditing, and a cross-modal CLIP coherence protocol, and apply it to AmaraSpatial-10K alongside matched subsets of Objaverse, HSSD, ABO, and GSO. We check the annotations independently of the LLM/VLM pipeline that produced them: measured ABO object heights fall inside our scale intervals 88.7-90.7% of the time, and three non-author raters auditing a stratified sample of 314 assets judge 95.5% plausibly scaled and 99.7% correctly anchored. AmaraSpatial-10K improves CLIP Recall@5 by 3.4x over Objaverse (0.612 vs. 0.181, median rank 267 -> 3), achieves a 99.1% settling-stability rate under Habitat-Sim in static placement and collision tests, with ~20x lower per-load wall time, and as a drop-in Holodeck asset bank improves planned-scale matching and is preferred by blinded human raters in 27 of 40 paired scenes. Controlled ablations on the same asset bank attribute the retrieval gain to description richness.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track)</p></details> |
| **[Closing the empirical loop: autonomous AI agents conduct end-to-end research with human participants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13421v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence systems are transforming scientific discovery by accelerating specific research tasks, from protein structure prediction to materials design, yet remain confined to narrow domains requiring substantial human oversight. Exponential growth of scientific literature and increasing domain specialisation constrain researchers capacity to synthesise knowledge across disciplines and develop unifying theories, motivating exploration of more general-purpose AI systems for science. Here we show that a domain-agnostic, agentic AI system can independently navigate the scientific workflow, from hypothesis generation through data collection to manuscript preparation. The system autonomously designed and executed three psychological studies on visual working memory, mental rotation, and imagery vividness, executed online data collection with 288 participants, developed analysis pipelines through over 8-hour continuous coding sessions, and produced completed manuscripts. The results demonstrate the capability of AI scientific discovery pipelines to conduct research with theoretical reasoning and methodological rigour comparable to experienced researchers, though with limitations in conceptual nuance and theoretical interpretation. This is a step toward embodied AI that can test hypotheses through real-world experiments, accelerating discovery by autonomously exploring regions of scientific space that human cognitive and resource constraints might otherwise leave unexplored. It raises important questions about the nature of scientific understanding and attribution of scientific credit.</p></details> |  |
| **[EAST: Embodied AI Safety Testing via Systematic Fault Injection in Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34358v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Task success is not a sufficient measure of safety for embodied artificial intelligence (AI), because robots may complete objectives while violating safety requirements. Existing evaluations often emphasize performance and robustness, but lack a systematic route from documented safety hazards to executable, verifiable tests. We present EAST (Embodied AI Safety Testing), a digital-twin framework that converts literature-grounded safety evidence into fault-injection tests across perception, instructions, environments, actions, and timing. EAST combines an expert-reviewed safety-fault taxonomy and eight executable operators with applicability and validity constraints. Its three-stage workflow generates test specifications, verifies fault realization during simulated execution, and independently assesses safety challenges, violations, task outcomes, and behavioral signatures using simulator-state evidence. The framework preserves execution traces and replayable records, supporting attribution and reproducibility across experiments. We evaluate EAST on six RoboTwin manipulation tasks and two policy architectures, ACT and RDT, against alternative testing strategies under matched budgets. Across five comparative campaigns, EAST targets all 74 admissible task-policy-operator combinations and obtains valid evidence for 63, while achieving safety-challenge exposure rates close to Random-Legal and Rule-Based. Moreover, 82 valid EAST executions complete their tasks despite safety violations, revealing risks concealed by success-oriented evaluation. These findings support systematic, validity-aware, reproducible safety testing beyond task-completion metrics.</p></details> |  |
| **[3D Generation for Embodied AI and Robotic Simulation: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26509v4)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI and robotic systems increasingly depend on scalable, diverse, and physically grounded 3D content for simulation-based training and real-world deployment. While 3D generative modeling has advanced rapidly, embodied applications impose requirements far beyond visual realism: generated objects must carry kinematic structure and material properties, scenes must support interaction and task execution, and the resulting content must bridge the gap between simulation and reality. This survey reviews 3D generation for embodied AI and organizes the literature around three roles that 3D generation plays in embodied systems. In Data Generator, 3D generation produces simulation-ready objects and assets, including articulated, physically grounded, and deformable content for downstream interaction; in Simulation Environments, it constructs interactive and task-oriented worlds, spanning structure-aware, controllable, and agentic scene generation; and in Sim2Real Bridge, it supports digital twin reconstruction, data augmentation, and synthetic demonstrations for downstream robot learning and real-world transfer. We also show that the field is shifting from visual realism toward interaction readiness, and we identify the main bottlenecks, including limited physical annotations, the gap between geometric quality and physical validity, fragmented evaluation, and the persistent sim-to-real divide, that must be addressed for 3D generation to become a dependable foundation for embodied intelligence. Our project page is at https://3dgen4robot.github.io.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages. For paper updates, please refer to https://3dgen4robot.github.io</p></details> |
| **[rMuscle: Robotic Muscle Memory for Efficient Vision-Language-Action Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19104v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Factory work is a promising early scenario for embodied AI: assigning repetitive manual jobs to robots has clear economic payoff, and a structured station keeps the jobs tractable for current policies. Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models now dominate as the policy paradigm for these robots. The inference latency of VLA models directly affects robot responsiveness and motion smoothness. However, existing VLA inference frameworks do not fully exploit the characteristics of embodied workloads or account for the distinct bottlenecks across different stages of VLA inference. In this paper, we first characterize embodied workloads and identify substantial task similarity across repeated robot executions. | **[World2HOSI: A Multi-Expert Harness for Long-Horizon Human-Object-Scene Interaction Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27725v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon human-object-scene interaction (HOSI) is central to embodied AI, animation, and game development. | **[Physical AI: Concepts, Techniques, and Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27045v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recent years, physical artificial intelligence (physical AI) has experienced rapid development, capturing significant attention in academia and industry. To address this gap, this monograph seeks to define physical AI, present its technical landscape, and examine each key technical subfield in depth. In doing so, it establishes a unified framework for Physical AI: artificial systems that perceive, model, and act upon the physical world through continuous feedback under real-world constraints. Further, this monograph distinguishes physical AI from conventional digital AI, embodied AI, cyber-physical systems, and digital twins. It develops the foundations of physical AI across physics, control, cognition, learning, and reasoning. It reviews key methodologies, including multimodal perception, physical data and simulation, world models, embodied foundation models, motor-control systems, multi-agent coordination, and hardware-software co-design. Furthermore, it examines applications spanning robotics, manufacturing, infrastructure, scientific experimentation, and autonomous services. Particular attention is given to sim-to-real transfer, reliability, real-time execution, evaluation, safety, governance, and long-term deployment. By connecting perception, prediction, decision-making, action, and adaptation in a closed loop, this monograph identifies the technical challenges and research directions required to advance from narrow automation toward more generalizable physical AI and, ultimately, physical artificial general intelligence (Physical AGI).</p></details> | <details><summary>Monog...</summary><p>Monograph, 12 chapters, 388 pages, 64 figures</p></details> |
| **[ForagerSim: Evaluating Embodied Map-Based Reasoning in Large-Scale Dynamic Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26395v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Animals build cognitive maps of vast environments, use them to perform map-based navigation, and might even be reusing the same circuitry used in spatial reasoning for creating and using maps of arbitrary task-spaces. | **[Compile Once, Run Cheap: Frozen, Portable Skills for Embodied Agents, and What Recursive Self-Improvement Adds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25114v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI lacks the data-rich pretraining that gave language models their agents and skills, so new manipulation capability costs demonstrations per task or a frontier model in every episode. Planning toward this goal is then organized as a graph of rule-specific nodes, where higher-level nodes inherit the union of their parents' rules, and candidate plans are progressively verified and aggregated until a single plan satisfies every relevant rule. We evaluate four tasks in LIVIN: 3D detection, 3D reconstruction, navigation, and loco-manipulation. Our evaluations show that current methods remain challenged by the dense object arrangements, occlusions, limited free space, and constrained interaction regions found in realistic lived-in homes. We hope LIVIN will help advance embodied AI in real-world homes, from spatial understanding to robotic interaction, and ultimately bring embodied intelligence into everyday home environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[USER: A Unified and Extensible System for Real-World Online Policy Learning in Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07837v5)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online policy learning directly in the physical world is a promising yet challenging direction for embodied intelligence. On the systems side, USER introduces a hardware abstraction layer for unified robot management and an adaptive communication plane that enables efficient cloud-edge training. On the learning side, USER adopts a fully asynchronous training framework, designs a persistent and cache-aware replay buffer, and provides extensible abstractions for rewards, algorithms, and policies. Experiments in both simulation and the real world demonstrate that USER supports multi-robot coordination, heterogeneous manipulators, cloud-edge training with large models, and long-running asynchronous training. | **[Generative AI for Autonomous Driving: Frontiers and Opportunities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08854v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) constitutes a transformative technological wave that reconfigures industries through its unparalleled capabilities for content creation, reasoning, planning, and multimodal understanding. We begin by distilling the principles and trade-offs of modern generative modeling, encompassing VAEs, GANs, Diffusion Models, and Large Language Models (LLMs). We then map their frontier applications in image, LiDAR, trajectory, occupancy, video generation as well as LLM-guided reasoning and decision making. We categorize practical applications, such as synthetic data workflows, end-to-end driving strategies, high-fidelity digital twin systems, smart transportation networks, and cross-domain transfer to embodied AI. We identify key obstacles and possibilities such as comprehensive generalization across rare cases, evaluation and safety checks, budget-limited implementation, regulatory compliance, ethical concerns, and environmental effects, while proposing research plans across theoretical assurances, trust metrics, transport integration, and socio-technical influence. By unifying these threads, the survey provides a forward-looking reference for researchers, engineers, and policymakers navigating the convergence of generative AI and advanced autonomous mobility. | **[BirdsongChat: A Hybrid Multi-Agent Framework for Multimodal Embodied Behavior Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20887v1)** | 2026-09-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal embodied systems require translating human intentions into interpretable and coordinated behaviors across heterogeneous modalities. | **[In-Context Robot Learning with VLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19138v1)** | 2026-09-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enabling robots to adapt to unfamiliar environments as readily as humans remains a moonshot goal of embodied AI. | **[A Comprehensive Review of Generative Physical Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18111v1)** | 2026-09-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of large-scale foundation models with physical embodiments has led to significant advancements in robotics known as Generative Physical Artificial Intelligence (GPAI). We introduce a taxonomy of five distinct approaches: Robot Foundation Models (RFMs) for cross-platform skill transfer; Vision-Language Action (VLA) models for end-to-end multi-modal perception and control; Large Behavior Models (LBMs) for human-like movement generation; Diffusion Policy Models (DPMs) for diffusion model-based temporally coherent action generation; and World Foundation Models (WFMs) for physics-compliant simulation and data generation. Through examples across autonomous vehicles, industrial automation, healthcare robotics, and humanoid systems, we identify significant performance improvements and summarize promising research directions in data-efficient learning, sim-to-real transfer, edge-compatible architectures, and safety frameworks. | **[The Robot Data Factory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16705v1)** | 2026-09-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Physical AI requires more than increasingly large robot datasets: intelligent robots acquire knowledge through continuous interaction with the physical world. | **[OJOx: Specification-Conditioned Demonstrations for Embodied AI in Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22289v1)** | 2026-09-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large-scale egocentric and whole-body human demonstrations are becoming a primary source of data for embodied intelligence. | **[NutriBench-Kitchen: Benchmarking Embodied AI for Nutrition Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07135v1)** | 2026-09-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An embodied kitchen assistant must do more than recognize food in isolated frames. | **[Rethinking Safety for Generalist Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06326v1)** | 2026-09-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generalist robots promise to transform our society: the same system that prepares a meal or folds laundry might also repair a car, inspect infrastructure, or care for a loved one. Yet this versatility introduces risks far beyond the collision- and force-based safety notions that have long dominated robotics. Notions of safety must now consider context (e.g., turning off a building's electricity is only safe during scheduled maintenance), user intent (e.g., asking the robot to ``clean the kitchen'' includes unspoken expectations that the robot should not mix dangerous but powerful cleaning agents like bleach and ammonia), hard-to-model physical consequences (e.g., burning food during meal preparation), and more. We argue the need for a new era of robot safety---embodied AI safety---that broadens the hazards considered across the robot's lifecycle while recognizing that the safety of bits cannot be separated from the safety of atoms. We present a taxonomy of emerging risks and a full-stack research agenda to guide the safe deployment of generalist robots.</p></details> | 11 pages, 2 figures |

| **[PolicyIK: High-Precision Inverse Kinematics for Robot Arms with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05372v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inverse kinematics (IK) converts end-effector pose commands into joint motion, and many vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely on it to act on a given arm and to transfer across embodiments. Precise IK remains laborious: redundancy and singularities complicate the inverse mapping, execution depends on hand-tuned controllers, and training data on physical robots are costly to collect. We present PolicyIK, a goal-conditioned reinforcement learning policy for high-precision IK. It updates joint-position references from pose feedback and observes its accumulated reference relative to the measured joints, so it can retain the offset that the position controller needs at rest. Two-scale pose errors with an error-dependent action gain resolve millimeter residuals while preserving large motions. Progress and dwell rewards teach it to both reach the tolerance and stay within it. In simulation, a single attempt by the trained PPO policy keeps the mean errors over the final second within 1 mm and 0.2° on 93.5% of held-out tasks, and a deployment protocol raises success to 98.9%. When trained directly on a physical arm with an off-policy RL learner, PolicyIK achieves higher reaching and holding success than all four classical IK controllers under the same time budgets. In simulated pick-and-place, PolicyIK executes the end-effector commands of the $π_{0.5}$ VLA policy with 95.7% success. These results show that reinforcement learning can provide high-precision inverse kinematics for robot arms. Code is available at https://github.com/Tinaliu0123/PolicyIK.</p></details> |  |
| **[Suppressing Misaligned Behaviors in Language Models, Durably](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05358v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Undesired model behaviors can emerge or re-emerge at any stage of training. Once observed, how effectively can we suppress them? To study this question, we first construct two model organisms--language models deliberately trained to exhibit a specific misalignment--that differ in what triggers the behavior. The narrow-trigger organism misbehaves only when the prompt contains a specific cue: it cheats on multiple-choice questions by following a planted hint that points to a wrong answer, and behaves normally otherwise. The broad-trigger organism has no such cue: after fine-tuning on insecure code, it gives harmful responses across a wide range of open-ended prompts. We then formalize post-hoc behavioral suppression and evaluate methods along three criteria: immediate suppression of the target behavior, durability under further training, and preservation of general capability. We compare standard methods--SFT, GRPO, and gradient-ascent unlearning--along these dimensions. Building on prior work on alignment shallowness, we hypothesize that more durable suppression requires the ability to recover from a wider range of misalignment states. We introduce Prefix-Expanded Adversarial Reinforcement Learning (PEARL), a GRPO variant that augments rollouts with continuations from intermediate states sampled along cached organism trajectories. Across both organism settings and two model families (Qwen3-4B, GPT-OSS-20B), PEARL achieves strong immediate suppression while preserving task accuracy, and remains durable under further fine-tuning on either the organism's data or unrelated data. It obtains the greatest benefit when the undesirable behavior is broad and cannot be addressed through targeted SFT. | **[Online Draft Co-Training for Speculative Decoding in Large-Scale, Long-Context RL Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07108v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Speculative decoding accelerates rollout generation, which dominates the cost of reinforcement learning (RL) post-training. However, training large models on long contexts requires context parallelism (CP) and pipeline parallelism (PP), and co-training drafts under this layout poses two obstacles: (1) the draft's branch attention is unsupported by causal CP, and (2) the target features that the draft consumes are spread across PP stages. In this paper, we present the design and implementation of online draft co-training in NeMo-RL. For CP, we extend packed, load-balanced zigzag ring attention by merging rank-local branch attention with causal main-sequence attention. For PP, TapChannel transports intermediate target features across stages via a separate path, leaving the pipeline schedule unaffected. Experiments show that co-training keeps the policy's reward and accuracy close to the no-draft baseline while delivering $1.48$--$2.25\times$ rollout speedups and $1.19$--$1.88\times$ end-to-end training speedups across model scales up to 122B. Our CP design achieves strong scaling at 256K sequence length with significant memory savings over prior work, and our PP transport incurs modest overhead. Code can be found at https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/RL/issues/3698.</p></details> | Technical Report |
| **[LenGuard-GPC: Length Guarding with Guided-Prompt Consistency for Spatial Reasoning Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17243v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-view spatial reasoning requires vision-language models to compare visual evidence across images, align object correspondences, and infer spatial relations over long visual contexts, a setting where chain-of-thought reasoning tends to grow verbose without becoming more accurate. For each sampled trajectory, it compares the token-wise predictive distributions under a standard prompt and a guided prompt, and uses the resulting token-sum KL divergence as a dense reward signal. Since this KL penalty accumulates over tokens and would otherwise reward shorter responses regardless of their quality, we introduce a staged length bonus that keeps reasoning length within a controlled range without simply encouraging brevity. On six multi-view spatial reasoning benchmarks, LenGuard-GPC improves accuracy over vanilla GRPO while reducing average response length.</p></details> |  |

Modeling RLVR as a Markov kernel, Jensen's inequality shows that conditional dispersion depresses pass@$k$ for $k\ge2$. Once dispersion is modeled, the conditional-mean log-odds slope is below one in every pair: RLVR improves hard prompts on average, while late-$k$ loss comes from a 2%-3% minority of base-solved prompts collapsing onto a repeated modal wrong answer and resisting prefix rescue up to 4,096 tokens. | **[Requests of a Feather Must Flock Together: Batch Size vs. Prefix Homogeneity in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06046v4)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Auto-regressive token generation in large language models is memory-bound because it requires "attending to" key and value tensors (KV cache) of all previous tokens. The key observation of our work is that with prefix-sharing workloads, smaller, prefix-homogeneous batches - where all requests share a common prefix - can achieve higher decode throughput than larger, heterogeneous batches, due to better spatial and temporal locality during KV cache accesses. However, current prefix-aware schedulers maximize prefix reuse within a batch only to reduce KV cache memory footprint, but do not stop batch formation at smaller homogeneous batches that could have performed better. Further, we show that shared prefix detection in existing schedulers relies on radix-tree traversals, incurring substantial CPU overhead that is often comparable to GPU execution time. This paper presents Feather, a prefix-aware scheduler that uses reinforcement learning (RL) to learn the optimal tradeoff between batch size and prefix homogeneity. We also introduce Chunked Hash Tree (CHT), a lightweight data structure that enables fast prefix detection and efficient request selection for the RL scheduler, avoiding expensive tree traversals. We integrate Feather into vLLM and SGLang, and our evaluation shows that Feather achieves 2-10$\times$ higher end-to-end throughput as compared to existing schedulers by reducing the total number of KV cache accesses, while doing no worse than the status quo when there is insufficient prefix sharing.</p></details> | 24 pages, 37 figures |

Our analysis identifies the root cause in the routing mechanism unique to MoE: because the router makes discrete expert-selection decisions, training-inference policy alignment becomes acutely sensitive to whether the same recovery technique is applied symmetrically on the training and inference sides. When an asymmetric recipe is used-including the dense-model approach, which corrects only the rollout-training-inference alignment deteriorates sharply, as measured by the Pearson correlation coefficient between training and inference probabilities, and router selection matching rates. Building on this finding, we propose MoeResQ, a quantization-recovery recipe for MoE RL post-training that enforces symmetry between the training- and inference-side corrections. | **[Scaling Nonlinear Optimization: Many Problems One GPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26341v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern GPUs and software frameworks have made sampling-based methods, such as reinforcement learning and model predictive path integral control, increasingly popular in robotics. NLP optimizers were among the field's main workhorses, but were not designed for modern GPU hardware. Embedding NLPs such as trajectory optimization and inverse kinematics directly within each sample would be highly valuable, yet the only current option is to call a CPU solver per sample, possibly in parallel on a multi-core CPU. To address this issue, in this work we present jaxipm, the first GPU-batched solver for generally constrained nonlinear optimization problems. Our approach is a JAX implementation of the battle-tested IPOPT algorithm, facilitated by eliminating control flow through heterogeneous iteration fusion, and minimizing GPU idle time through iteration-level batching. Our implementation enables solving hundreds of NLPs in parallel on a single GPU. Complementing this, a state-anchor mechanism automatically annotates task-relevant UI elements in successful goal states as reference anchors. A same-reference human control does not exhibit these patterns; additional human audits examine the visual validity of the induced labels. Motivated by this diagnosis, we propose PAGER (Position-Aware Grounded Evidence Reinforcement), a GRPO training objective with separate signals for supported fine-grained middle evidence, evidence-bearing tail continuation, claim coverage, and content-aware length control. Certifying Declared Change Envelopes for Model Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04192v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deployed language models are updated continually, and regulators increasingly accept changes that were declared in advance. We formalize the problem with declared change envelopes. Before training, the developer commits to a region of inputs, and every decision that changes outside it counts as leakage. Leakage compares two models, so it can be certified from unlabeled traffic with anytime-valid tests whose sample cost we bound from above and below, and per-update certificates compose into a bound on lifetime drift outside all declared scopes. We measure leakage for LoRA and full fine-tuning, preference optimization, reinforcement learning, and knowledge editing on models from 1.5B to 72B parameters. Narrow updates leak widely: domain and policy LoRA updates keep only about 7-36% of the decisions they change inside the declared scope. Across 256 trained variants, leakage tracks the update's KL divergence to the incumbent on out-of-scope inputs (Spearman 0.94). None of the customary remedies we tested makes a rule-like update certifiable without giving up much of its intended change. Distilling the incumbent's decisions on unlabeled prompts in the deployment format does: anchored LoRA, GRPO, and (at a small learning rate) full fine-tuning updates certify 5% leakage from a few hundred to a few thousand unlabeled samples, and the certificates replicate on held-out traffic and on written answers. Code: https://github.com/Usama1002/declared-change-envelopes</p></details> |  |

In this work, we introduce a tractable alignment score and derive its closed-form update during fine-tuning, yielding a unified framework for alignment dynamics. Our analysis decomposes alignment updates into two competing components: a \textbf{\color{red!60!black} Rebound Force}, governed jointly by the current alignment state and the narrowness of model distribution, and a \textbf{\color{green!60!black} Driving Force}, determined by how the training distribution aligns with outcome-conditioned posteriors over aligned and non-aligned completions. This decomposition explains why prior alignment can be reversed by later fine-tuning and why narrower posterior structure strengthens such reversal. Moreover, our framework predicts a \textbf{Rehearsal Priming Effect}: prior alignment leaves a latent posterior imprint that amplifies the effective Driving Force upon re-exposure, leading to faster re-alignment. We validate these predictions across safety alignment, emergent misalignment, and sentiment settings, demonstrating consistent alignment reversal and accelerated re-alignment under re-exposure. In addition, controlled experiments in safety alignment confirm the predicted dependence of rebound strength on posterior narrowness. Together, these results provide a unified dynamical perspective on how alignment is disrupted and reactivated during LLM fine-tuning.</p></details> |  |

FPGA synthesis results demonstrate that BRL-S reduces lookup-table utilization by approximately 68--72% compared with RL-S. ASIC synthesis further shows reductions of 63--67% in area and 64--69% in estimated power consumption. These results demonstrate that bucket-based scheduling substantially improves the hardware efficiency of reinforcement-learned QLDPC decoders.</p></details> |  |

| **[ModelBlaster: Closing the Performance Gap through Learning-Augmented Agents Across Models, Kernels and Hardware Backends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04104v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Optimizing accelerator code across hardware architectures is difficult. Empirical results demonstrate that our method calibrates zero-shot estimates and mitigates confirmation bias, providing fine-grained, partial progress metrics that accurately penalize failed executions while correctly interpolating successful runs. | **[PolicyIK: High-Precision Inverse Kinematics for Robot Arms with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05372v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inverse kinematics (IK) converts end-effector pose commands into joint motion, and many vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely on it to act on a given arm and to transfer across embodiments. We present PolicyIK, a goal-conditioned reinforcement learning policy for high-precision IK. It updates joint-position references from pose feedback and observes its accumulated reference relative to the measured joints, so it can retain the offset that the position controller needs at rest. Two-scale pose errors with an error-dependent action gain resolve millimeter residuals while preserving large motions. Progress and dwell rewards teach it to both reach the tolerance and stay within it. In simulation, a single attempt by the trained PPO policy keeps the mean errors over the final second within 1 mm and 0.2° on 93.5% of held-out tasks, and a deployment protocol raises success to 98.9%. When trained directly on a physical arm with an off-policy RL learner, PolicyIK achieves higher reaching and holding success than all four classical IK controllers under the same time budgets. In simulated pick-and-place, PolicyIK executes the end-effector commands of the $π_{0.5}$ VLA policy with 95.7% success. These results show that reinforcement learning can provide high-precision inverse kinematics for robot arms. Code is available at https://github.com/Tinaliu0123/PolicyIK.</p></details> |  |
| **[Pretraining Knows What, Simulation Teaches How: Compositional Generalization for Agentic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35817v3)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier models such as GPT-6 Astra now directly control robots and can generalize better than vision-language-action models (VLAs). We show that small open VLMs are already capable of the first and mainly lack the second. ZAP reaches 96.7% success on real-world tasks, matching GPT-6 Astra in our harness while running 3x faster and 17x cheaper, and 74.0% on a bimanual adaptation of RoboLab-120, on par with GPT-6 Astra's 72.2%. | **[PyRUA-DataFarm: LLM Agent as a Cost-Efficient Teleoperator for Real-Ready Robot Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05320v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teleoperation remains the default way to collect robot demonstrations, but every episode costs robot and operator time, and every new task starts from scratch. The simulator supplies exact actions, and a video model conditioned on real images renders each rollout as photographic video, generating all camera views together consistently. The output is real-ready data: synchronized camera views labelled with the robot's actions, on which a policy can be trained directly and deployed on the real robot. One hour of three-view data for a task costs \$107 on average, these costs included, slightly below a quote of \$117 for teleoperation in China and below \$290 in labour alone in North America.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 9 figures, 9 tables. Project page: https://dagroup-pku.github.io/PyRUA-Lean/DataFarm Code: https://github.com/DAGroup-PKU/PyRUA-Lean/tree/DataFarm</p></details> |

| **[Haptic Shared Control for Multi-Robot Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05027v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems offer significant advantages in terms of flexibility and redundancy, enabling the efficient distribution of complex tasks among multiple robots. | **[Pixel: A Co-Designed Full-Stack Robot System for Learning General-Purpose Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03648v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>General-purpose mobile manipulation requires a combination of capable robot hardware, scalable demonstration collection, expressive whole-body control, and effective policy learning. | **[Robot-Matched Soft Fingers for UMI Data Collection: Lessons from 100 Hours of Pick and Place](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03779v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training robot policies for multi-task pick and place requires demonstrations on many objects, which handheld Universal Manipulation Interfaces (UMIs) can collect quickly and cheaply. | **[Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30613v5)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Changes to what a robot can see do not mean that there is a change in the problem it is trying to solve, and many language-conditioned manipulation systems struggle with this distinction. Code and videos can be found at https://cwru-aism.github.io/spark-page/</p></details> | 30 pages, 7 figures |

Project website: https://younghyopark.me/inertia-mismatch/</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 17 figures. Project website: https://younghyopark.me/inertia-mismatch/</p></details> |
